The following description:

## mseccfg register itself

- `mseccfg` is a 64-bit read/write register, formatted as shown in Figure 26, that controls security features.
- Figure 26: Machine security configuration (`mseccfg`) register. [The supplied text contains an image placeholder for this figure; no additional textual bit layout is provided in the supplied text.]
- `mseccfg` exists if any extension that adds a field to `mseccfg` is implemented. Otherwise, it is reserved.
- When XLEN=32 only, `mseccfgh` is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of `mseccfg`. Register `mseccfgh` exists when XLEN=32 and `mseccfg` is implemented; it does not exist when XLEN=64.

## Fields added by Zkr: SSEED and USEED

- The Zkr extension adds the `SSEED` and `USEED` fields to the `mseccfg` CSR to control access to the `seed` CSR from modes less privileged than M.
- When `USEED` is 0, access to the `seed` CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
- When `USEED` is 1, read-write access to the `seed` CSR from U-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, `USEED` is read-only zero.
- When `SSEED` is 0, access to the `seed` CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
- When `SSEED` is 1, read-write access to the `seed` CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, `SSEED` is read-only zero.
- When the H extension is also implemented, access to the `seed` CSR from an HS-qualified instruction leads to a virtual-instruction exception in VS and VU modes; all other types of accesses raise an illegal-instruction exception.

Table 20. Entropy Source Access Control.

| Mode   | SSEED | USEED | Description |
|--------|-------|-------|-------------|
| M      | -     | -     | The seed CSR is always available in machine mode as normal (with a CSR read-write instruction.) Attempted read without a write raises an illegal-instruction exception regardless of mode and access control bits. |
| U      | -     | 0     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| U      | -     | 1     | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write. |
| S/HS   | 0     | -     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| S/HS   | 1     | -     | The seed CSR is accessible as normal. No exception is raised for read-write. |
| VS/VU  | 0     | -     | Any seed CSR access raises an illegal-instruction exception. |
| VS/VU  | 1     | -     | A read-write seed access raises a virtual-instruction exception, while other access conditions raise an illegal-instruction exception. |

## Fields added by Smepmp: RLB, MMWP, and MML

- The Smepmp extension adds the `RLB`, `MMWP`, and the `MML` fields in `mseccfg`.

### mseccfg.RLB

- `mseccfg.RLB` (Rule Locking Bypass): a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify Locked PMP rules.
- When `mseccfg.RLB` is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.
- When `mseccfg.RLB` is 0 and `pmpcfg.L` is 1 in any rule or entry (including disabled entries), then `mseccfg.RLB` remains 0 and any further modifications to `mseccfg.RLB` are ignored until a PMP reset.
- This feature is intended to be used as a debug mechanism, or as a temporary workaround during the boot process for simplifying software, and optimizing the allocation of memory and PMP rules.
- Using this functionality under normal operation, after the boot process is completed, should be avoided since it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.
- Vendors who don't need this functionality may hardwire this field to 0.

### mseccfg.MMWP

- The `mseccfg.MMWP` (Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy) is a WARL field.
- This field changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule.
- This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
- When set it changes the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that don't have a matching PMP rule, to denied instead of ignored.

### mseccfg.MML

- The `mseccfg.MML` (Machine Mode Lockdown) is a WARL field.
- The `MML` bit changes the interpretation of the `pmpcfg.L` bit defined in Section 3.7.1.2.
- This is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
- When `mseccfg.MML` is set the system's behavior changes in the following way:
  1. The meaning of `pmpcfg.L` changes: Instead of marking a rule as locked and enforced in all modes, it now marks a rule as M-mode-only when set and S/U-mode-only when unset. The formerly reserved encoding of `pmpcfg.RW=01`, and the encoding `pmpcfg.LRWX=1111`, now encode a Shared-Region.
     - An M-mode-only rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode. It also remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless `mseccfg.RLB` is set.
     - An S/U-mode-only rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.
     - A Shared-Region rule is enforced on all modes, with restrictions depending on the `pmpcfg.L` and `pmpcfg.X` bits:
       - A Shared-Region rule where `pmpcfg.L` is not set can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode, so is not executable. M-mode has read/write access to that region, and S/U-mode has read access if `pmpcfg.X` is not set, or read/write access if `pmpcfg.X` is set.
       - A Shared-Region rule where `pmpcfg.L` is set can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode, so is not writable. Both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access on the region, and M-mode also has read access if `pmpcfg.X` is set. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless `mseccfg.RLB` is set.
       - The encoding `pmpcfg.LRWX=1111` can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U mode, where both modes only have read-only access to the region. The rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset, unless `mseccfg.RLB` is set.
  2. Adding a rule with executable privileges that either is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region is not possible and such `pmpcfg` writes are ignored, leaving `pmpcfg` unchanged. This restriction can be temporarily lifted by setting `mseccfg.RLB` e.g. during the boot process.
  3. Executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges. Executing code from a region without a matching rule or with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.
  4. If `mseccfg.MML` is not set, the combination of `pmpcfg.RW=01` remains reserved for future standard use.

## PMM field

- If the Smmpm extension is implemented, the `PMM` field enables or disables pointer masking (see Chapter 25) for M-mode according to the values in Table 21.
- If Smmpm is not implemented, `PMM` is read-only zero.
- The `PMM` field is read-only zero for RV32.

Table 21. Legal values of PMM WARL field

| Value | Description |
|-------|-------------|
| 00    | Pointer masking is disabled (PMLEN = 0) |
| 01    | Reserved |
| 10    | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 57 (PMLEN = 7 on RV64) |
| 11    | Pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN = XLEN - 48 (PMLEN = 16 on RV64) |

- Smmpm implementations need to satisfy max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.

## MLPE field

- The Zicfilp extension adds the `MLPE` field in `mseccfg`.
- When `MLPE` field is 1, Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
- When the `MLPE` field is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode and the following rules apply to M-mode:
  - The hart does not update the ELP state; it remains as `NO_LP_EXPECTED`.
  - The `LPAD` instruction operates as a no-op.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mseccfg is a 64-bit read/write register that controls security features.
RULE 2: mseccfg exists if any extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 3: mseccfg is reserved if no extension that adds a field to mseccfg is implemented.
RULE 4: When XLEN=32, mseccfgh is a 32-bit read/write register that aliases bits 63:32 of mseccfg.
RULE 5: When XLEN=32 and mseccfg is implemented, mseccfgh exists.
RULE 6: When XLEN=64, the mseccfgh alias register of mseccfg does not exist.
RULE 7: The Zkr extension adds the SSEED and USEED fields to mseccfg to control access to the seed CSR from modes less privileged than M.
RULE 8: When mseccfg.USEED is 0, access to the seed CSR in U-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from U-mode is allowed.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.USEED is 1, a seed CSR access from U-mode that is not a read-write access raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 11: If Zkr or U-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.USEED is read-only zero.
RULE 12: When mseccfg.SSEED is 0, access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, read-write access to the seed CSR from S-/HS-mode is allowed.
RULE 14: When mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a seed CSR access from S-/HS-mode that is not a read-write access raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 15: If Zkr or S-mode is not implemented, mseccfg.SSEED is read-only zero.
RULE 16: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 0, any seed CSR access from VS or VU mode raises an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 17: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, a read-write seed CSR access from VS or VU mode by an HS-qualified instruction raises a virtual-instruction exception.
RULE 18: When the H extension is implemented and mseccfg.SSEED is 1, all other types of seed CSR accesses from VS or VU mode raise an illegal-instruction exception.
RULE 19: The Smepmp extension adds the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in mseccfg.
RULE 20: mseccfg.RLB (Rule Locking Bypass) is a WARL field that provides a mechanism to temporarily modify Locked PMP rules.
RULE 21: When mseccfg.RLB is 1, locked PMP rules may be removed or modified and locked PMP rules may be edited.
RULE 22: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry including disabled entries, mseccfg.RLB remains 0.
RULE 23: When mseccfg.RLB is 0 and pmpcfg.L is 1 in any PMP rule or entry including disabled entries, any further modifications to mseccfg.RLB are ignored until a PMP reset.
RULE 24: Using mseccfg.RLB under normal operation after the boot process is completed should be avoided because it weakens the protection of M-mode-only rules.
RULE 25: mseccfg.MMWP (Machine-Mode Allowlist Policy) is a WARL field.
RULE 26: mseccfg.MMWP changes the default PMP policy for Machine mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule.
RULE 27: mseccfg.MMWP is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
RULE 28: When mseccfg.MMWP is set, the default PMP policy for M-mode when accessing memory regions that do not have a matching PMP rule is denied instead of ignored.
RULE 29: mseccfg.MML (Machine Mode Lockdown) is a WARL field.
RULE 30: mseccfg.MML changes the interpretation of the pmpcfg.L bit.
RULE 31: mseccfg.MML is a sticky bit, meaning that once set it cannot be unset until a PMP reset.
RULE 32: When mseccfg.MML is set, pmpcfg.L set marks a PMP rule as M-mode-only instead of marking that rule as locked and enforced in all modes.
RULE 33: When mseccfg.MML is set, pmpcfg.L unset marks a PMP rule as S/U-mode-only.
RULE 34: When mseccfg.MML is set, the formerly reserved encoding pmpcfg.RW=01 encodes a Shared-Region.
RULE 35: When mseccfg.MML is set, the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 encodes a Shared-Region.
RULE 36: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only PMP rule is enforced on Machine mode and denied in Supervisor or User mode.
RULE 37: When mseccfg.MML is set, an M-mode-only PMP rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 38: When mseccfg.MML is set, an S/U-mode-only PMP rule is enforced on Supervisor and User modes and denied on Machine mode.
RULE 39: When mseccfg.MML is set, a Shared-Region PMP rule is enforced on all modes with restrictions depending on pmpcfg.L and pmpcfg.X.
RULE 40: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L not set, the rule can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode and is not executable.
RULE 41: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L not set, M-mode has read/write access to that region.
RULE 42: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L not set and pmpcfg.X is not set, S/U-mode has read access to that region.
RULE 43: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L not set and pmpcfg.X is set, S/U-mode has read/write access to that region.
RULE 44: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L set, the rule can be used for sharing code between M-mode and S/U-mode and is not writable.
RULE 45: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L set, both M-mode and S/U-mode have execute access to the region.
RULE 46: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L set and pmpcfg.X is set, M-mode also has read access to the region.
RULE 47: When mseccfg.MML is set and a Shared-Region PMP rule has pmpcfg.L set, the rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 48: When mseccfg.MML is set, the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111 can be used for sharing data between M-mode and S/U-mode with both modes having only read-only access to the region.
RULE 49: When mseccfg.MML is set and a PMP rule uses the encoding pmpcfg.LRWX=1111, the rule remains locked so that any further modifications to its associated configuration or address registers are ignored until a PMP reset unless mseccfg.RLB is set.
RULE 50: When mseccfg.MML is set, adding a PMP rule with executable privileges that is M-mode-only is not possible and such pmpcfg writes are ignored leaving pmpcfg unchanged.
RULE 51: When mseccfg.MML is set, adding a PMP rule with executable privileges that is a locked Shared-Region is not possible and such pmpcfg writes are ignored leaving pmpcfg unchanged.
RULE 52: When mseccfg.MML is set, the restriction on adding a PMP rule with executable privileges that is M-mode-only or a locked Shared-Region can be temporarily lifted by setting mseccfg.RLB, for example during the boot process.
RULE 53: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with Machine mode privileges is only possible from memory regions with a matching M-mode-only rule or a matching locked Shared-Region rule with executable privileges.
RULE 54: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with Machine mode privileges from a memory region without a matching PMP rule is denied.
RULE 55: When mseccfg.MML is set, executing code with Machine mode privileges from a memory region with a matching S/U-mode-only rule is denied.
RULE 56: When mseccfg.MML is not set, the combination pmpcfg.RW=01 remains reserved for future standard use.
RULE 57: mseccfg.PMM is a WARL field.
RULE 58: When Smmpm is implemented, mseccfg.PMM enables or disables pointer masking for M-mode according to its legal values.
RULE 59: If Smmpm is not implemented, mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero.
RULE 60: mseccfg.PMM is read-only zero for RV32.
RULE 61: When mseccfg.PMM is 00, pointer masking is disabled with PMLEN=0.
RULE 62: mseccfg.PMM value 01 is reserved.
RULE 63: When mseccfg.PMM is 10, pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN=XLEN-57, which is PMLEN=7 on RV64.
RULE 64: When mseccfg.PMM is 11, pointer masking is enabled with PMLEN=XLEN-48, which is PMLEN=16 on RV64.
RULE 65: For mseccfg.PMM, Smmpm implementations need to satisfy max(largest supported virtual address size, largest supported supervisor physical address size) <= (XLEN - PMLEN) bits to avoid any masking logic on the TLB access path.
RULE 66: The Zicfilp extension adds the MLPE field in mseccfg.
RULE 67: When mseccfg.MLPE is 1, the Zicfilp extension is enabled in M-mode.
RULE 68: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the Zicfilp extension is not enabled in M-mode.
RULE 69: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the hart does not update the ELP state and the ELP state remains NO_LP_EXPECTED in M-mode.
RULE 70: When mseccfg.MLPE is 0, the LPAD instruction operates as a no-op in M-mode.
======================================================= END - 4 ========================================================

The following description:

In Section 3.4 Reset:

- If the Zicfilp extension is implemented, the mseccfg .MLPE field is reset to 0.
- The MML, MMWP, and RLB fields of the mseccfg register are set to 0, unless the platform mandates a different reset value.
- The USEED and SSEED fields of the mseccfg CSR must have defined reset values.

RULE 3: At reset, the mseccfg.MMWP field is set to 0 unless the platform mandates a different reset value.

The following description:

- The extension introduces the RLB, MMWP, and MML fields in the mseccfg CSR and their associated rules, to address the threat model outlined in Section Section 6.1. See Figure 26 for the detailed specification of these fields and the corresponding rules.
- The physical memory protection rules when mseccfg.MML is set to 1 are summarized in the truth table below.
- The text states: "A visual representation of these rules is as follows:" and then includes an image.

Condition for all rows in the following table: mseccfg.MML is set to 1.

| Bits on pmpcfg L | Bits on pmpcfg R | Bits on pmpcfg W | Bits on pmpcfg X | Result MMode | Result S/U Mode |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) | Inaccessible region (Access Exception) |
| 0 | 0 | 0 | 1 | Access Exception | Execute-only region |
| 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write on Mmode, read-only on S/U mode |
| 0 | 0 | 1 | 1 | Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode | Shared data region: Read/write for both Mand S/U mode |
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 | Access Exception | Read-only region |
| 0 | 1 | 0 | 1 | Access Exception | Read/Execute region |
| 0 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Access Exception | Read/Write region |
| 0 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Access Exception | Read/Write/Execute region |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | 0 | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) | Locked inaccessible region* (Access Exception) |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | 1 | Locked Execute-only region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | 0 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on both Mand S/U mode.* |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | 1 | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* | Locked Shared code region: Execute only on S/U mode, read/execute on Mmode.* |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 | Locked Read-only region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | 1 | Locked Read/Execute region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Locked Read/Write region* | Access Exception |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | 1 | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.* | Locked Shared data region: Read only on both Mand S/U mode.* |
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The extension introduces the mseccfg.RLB field in the mseccfg CSR.
RULE 2: The extension introduces the mseccfg.MMWP field in the mseccfg CSR.
RULE 3: The extension introduces the mseccfg.MML field in the mseccfg CSR.
RULE 4: The extension introduces associated rules for the mseccfg.RLB, mseccfg.MMWP, and mseccfg.MML fields to address the threat model.
RULE 5: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1, the physical memory protection rules are expressed in terms of the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits and their M-mode and S/U-mode results.
RULE 6: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 0, 0, 0, the region is inaccessible with an access exception in M-mode and is inaccessible with an access exception in S/U-mode.
RULE 7: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 0, 0, 1, the M-mode result is an access exception and the S/U-mode result is an execute-only region.
RULE 8: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 0, 1, 0, the M-mode and S/U-mode results both specify a shared data region that is read/write in M-mode and read-only in S/U-mode.
RULE 9: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 0, 1, 1, the M-mode and S/U-mode results both specify a shared data region that is read/write for both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 10: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 1, 0, 0, the M-mode result is an access exception and the S/U-mode result is a read-only region.
RULE 11: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 1, 0, 1, the M-mode result is an access exception and the S/U-mode result is a read/execute region.
RULE 12: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 1, 1, 0, the M-mode result is an access exception and the S/U-mode result is a read/write region.
RULE 13: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 0, 1, 1, 1, the M-mode result is an access exception and the S/U-mode result is a read/write/execute region.
RULE 14: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 0, 0, 0, the region is locked inaccessible with an access exception in M-mode and is locked inaccessible with an access exception in S/U-mode.
RULE 15: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 0, 0, 1, the M-mode result is a locked execute-only region and the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 16: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 0, 1, 0, the M-mode and S/U-mode results both specify a locked shared code region that is execute-only in both M-mode and S/U-mode.
RULE 17: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 0, 1, 1, the M-mode and S/U-mode results both specify a locked shared code region that is execute-only in S/U-mode and read/execute in M-mode.
RULE 18: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 1, 0, 0, the M-mode result is a locked read-only region and the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 19: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 1, 0, 1, the M-mode result is a locked read/execute region and the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 20: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 1, 1, 0, the M-mode result is a locked read/write region and the S/U-mode result is an access exception.
RULE 21: When mseccfg.MML is set to 1 and the pmpcfg L, R, W, and X bits are 1, 1, 1, 1, the M-mode and S/U-mode results both specify a locked shared data region that is read-only in both M-mode and S/U-mode.

The following description:

- A vector context status field, VS, is added to mstatus[10:9] and shadowed in sstatus[10:9].
- It is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field, FS.
- Attempts to execute any vector instruction, or to access the vector CSRs, raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.
- When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
- Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.
- Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization.
- Software will typically use VS to reduce context-swap overhead.
- If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1; otherwise, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.
- Implementations may have a writable misa.V field.
- Analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled, the mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear.
- Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear, enables vector emulation and simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: A vector context status field VS is added to mstatus[10:9].
RULE 2: The mstatus.VS field is shadowed in sstatus[10:9].
RULE 3: The mstatus.VS field is defined analogously to the floating-point context status field FS.
RULE 4: Attempts to execute any vector instruction raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.
RULE 5: Attempts to access the vector CSRs raise an illegal-instruction exception when mstatus.VS is set to Off.
RULE 6: When mstatus.VS is set to Initial or Clean, executing any instruction that changes vector state, including the vector CSRs, will change mstatus.VS to Dirty.
RULE 7: Implementations may also change mstatus.VS from Initial or Clean to Dirty at any time, even when there is no change in vector state.
RULE 8: Accurate setting of mstatus.VS is an optimization.
RULE 9: Software will typically use mstatus.VS to reduce context-swap overhead.
RULE 10: If mstatus.VS is Dirty, mstatus.SD is 1.
RULE 11: If mstatus.VS is not Dirty, mstatus.SD is set in accordance with existing specifications.
RULE 12: The mstatus.VS field may exist even if misa.V is clear, analogous to the way in which the floating-point unit is handled.
RULE 13: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear enables vector emulation.
RULE 14: Allowing mstatus.VS to exist when misa.V is clear simplifies handling of mstatus.VS in systems with writable misa.V.

The following description:

- Global interrupt-enable bits, MIE and SIE, are provided for M-mode and S-mode respectively. These bits are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.

- The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.

- When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled when xIE=1 and globally disabled when xIE=0. Interrupts for lower-privilege modes, w < x, are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global wIE bit for the lower-privilege mode. Interrupts for higher-privilege modes, y > x, are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global yIE bit for the higher-privilege mode.

- If supervisor mode is not implemented, then SIE and SPIE are read-only 0.

- To support nested traps, each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts has a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes.

- xPIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to the trap, and xPP holds the previous privilege mode.

- The xPP fields can only hold privilege modes up to x, so MPP is two bits wide and SPP is one bit wide.

- When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, xPIE is set to the value of xIE; xIE is set to 0; and xPP is set to y.

- An MRET or SRET instruction is used to return from a trap in M-mode or S-mode respectively.

- When executing an xRET instruction, supposing xPP holds the value y, xIE is set to xPIE; the privilege mode is changed to y; xPIE is set to 1; and xPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode (U if U-mode is implemented, else M).

- When executing an xRET instruction, supposing xPP holds the value y, if y != M, xRET also sets MPRV=0.

- Setting xPP to the least-privileged supported mode on an xRET helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.

- xPP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.

- If privilege mode x is not implemented, then xPP must be read-only 0.

- M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to MPP then reading it back.

- If the machine provides only U and M modes, then only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in MPP.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mstatus provides the global interrupt-enable bits MIE and SIE for M-mode and S-mode, respectively.
RULE 2: mstatus.MIE and mstatus.SIE are primarily used to guarantee atomicity with respect to interrupt handlers in the current privilege mode.
RULE 3: The global xIE bits are located in the low-order bits of mstatus, allowing them to be atomically set or cleared with a single CSR instruction.
RULE 4: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally enabled when mstatus.xIE is 1.
RULE 5: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts are globally disabled when mstatus.xIE is 0.
RULE 6: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for lower-privilege modes w < x are always globally disabled regardless of the setting of any global wIE bit for the lower-privilege mode, including the mstatus wIE bit if one exists.
RULE 7: When a hart is executing in privilege mode x, interrupts for higher-privilege modes y > x are always globally enabled regardless of the setting of the global yIE bit for the higher-privilege mode, including the mstatus yIE bit if one exists.
RULE 8: If supervisor mode is not implemented, mstatus.SIE and mstatus.SPIE are read-only 0.
RULE 9: To support nested traps, mstatus contains, for each privilege mode x that can respond to interrupts, a two-level stack of interrupt-enable bits and privilege modes.
RULE 10: mstatus.xPIE holds the value of the interrupt-enable bit active prior to a trap.
RULE 11: mstatus.xPP holds the previous privilege mode.
RULE 12: mstatus.xPP fields can only hold privilege modes up to privilege mode x.
RULE 13: mstatus.MPP is two bits wide.
RULE 14: mstatus.SPP is one bit wide.
RULE 15: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, mstatus.xPIE is set to the value of mstatus.xIE.
RULE 16: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, mstatus.xIE is set to 0.
RULE 17: When a trap is taken from privilege mode y into privilege mode x, mstatus.xPP is set to y.
RULE 18: When executing an MRET or SRET instruction, supposing the corresponding mstatus.xPP holds the value y, the corresponding mstatus.xIE is set to the corresponding mstatus.xPIE.
RULE 19: When executing an MRET or SRET instruction, supposing the corresponding mstatus.xPP holds the value y, the privilege mode is changed to y.
RULE 20: When executing an MRET or SRET instruction, supposing the corresponding mstatus.xPP holds the value y, the corresponding mstatus.xPIE is set to 1.
RULE 21: When executing an MRET or SRET instruction, supposing the corresponding mstatus.xPP holds the value y, the corresponding mstatus.xPP is set to the least-privileged supported mode, which is U if U-mode is implemented and otherwise M.
RULE 22: When executing an MRET or SRET instruction, supposing the corresponding mstatus.xPP holds the value y and y is not M, that instruction also sets mstatus.MPRV to 0.
RULE 23: Setting mstatus.xPP to the least-privileged supported mode on an MRET or SRET instruction helps identify software bugs in the management of the two-level privilege-mode stack.
RULE 24: mstatus.xPP fields are WARL fields that can hold only privilege mode x and any implemented privilege mode lower than x.
RULE 25: If privilege mode x is not implemented, mstatus.xPP must be read-only 0.
RULE 26: M-mode software can determine whether a privilege mode is implemented by writing that mode to mstatus.MPP and then reading mstatus.MPP back.
RULE 27: If the machine provides only U and M modes, only a single hardware storage bit is required to represent either 00 or 11 in mstatus.MPP.

The following description:

- The M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension.
- Upon reset, the MDT field is set to 1.
- When the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE (Machine Interrupt Enable) bit is cleared to 0.
- For RV64, when the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, this clearing of the MIE bit to 0 occurs regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.
- The MIE bit can only be set to 1 by an explicit CSR write if the MDT bit is already 0 or, for RV64, is being set to 0 by the same write.
- For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit in mstatus register.
- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode, if the MDT bit is currently 0, it is then set to 1, and the trap is delivered as expected.
- When a trap is to be taken into M-mode and MDT is already set to 1, then this is an unexpected trap.
- When the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.
- A trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit.
- A trap that occurs when executing in M-mode with mnstatus.NMIE set to 0 is an unexpected trap.
- The MRET and SRET instructions, when executed in M-mode, set the MDT bit to 0.
- The MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.

RULE 1: For the mstatus register, the M-mode-disable-trap (MDT) bit is a WARL field introduced by the Smdbltrp extension.

RULE 3: For the mstatus register, when the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE bit in mstatus is cleared to 0.
RULE 4: For the mstatus register on RV64, when the MDT bit is set to 1 by an explicit CSR write, the MIE bit in mstatus is cleared to 0 regardless of the value written, if any, to the MIE bit by the same write.

RULE 6: For RV32, the MDT bit is in mstatush and the MIE bit is in the mstatus register.
RULE 7: For the mstatus register, when a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit is currently 0, the MDT bit is then set to 1 and the trap is delivered as expected.
RULE 8: For the mstatus register, when a trap is to be taken into M-mode and the MDT bit is already 1, that trap is an unexpected trap.
RULE 9: For the mstatus register, when the Smrnmi extension is implemented, a trap caused by an RNMI is not considered an unexpected trap irrespective of the state of the MDT bit.
RULE 10: For the mstatus register, a trap caused by an RNMI does not set the MDT bit.

RULE 12: For the mstatus register, the SRET instruction, when executed in M-mode, sets the MDT bit to 0.
RULE 13: For the mstatus register, the MNRET instruction, provided by the Smrnmi extension, sets the MDT bit to 0 if the new privilege mode is not M.

The following description:

- The section titled "Base ISA Control in mstatus Register" states: For RV64 harts, the SXL and UXL fields are WARL fields that control the value of XLEN for S-mode and Umode, respectively.
- The encoding of these fields is the same as the MXL field of misa.
- The effective XLEN in S-mode and U-mode are termed SXLEN and UXLEN, respectively.
- When MXLEN=32, the SXL and UXL fields do not exist, and SXLEN=32 and UXLEN=32.
- When MXLEN=64 and S-mode is not supported, then SXL is read-only zero.
- When MXLEN=64 and the condition "S-mode is not supported" does not hold, SXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of SXLEN.
- When MXLEN=64 and the condition "S-mode is not supported" does not hold, an implementation may make SXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that SXLEN=MXLEN.
- When MXLEN=64 and U-mode is not supported, then UXL is read-only zero.
- When MXLEN=64 and the condition "U-mode is not supported" does not hold, UXL is a WARL field that encodes the current value of UXLEN.
- When MXLEN=64 and the condition "U-mode is not supported" does not hold, an implementation may make UXL be a read-only field whose value always ensures that UXLEN=MXLEN or UXLEN=SXLEN.
- If S-mode is implemented, the set of legal values that the UXL field may assume excludes those that would cause UXLEN to be greater than SXLEN.
- Whenever XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, all operations must ignore source operand register bits above the configured XLEN, and must sign-extend results to fill the entire widest supported XLEN in the destination register.
- Similarly, when XLEN in any mode is set to a value less than the widest supported XLEN, pc bits above XLEN are ignored, and when the pc is written, it is sign-extended to fill the widest supported XLEN.
- The source states: We require that operations always fill the entire underlying hardware registers with defined values to avoid implementation-defined behavior.
- The architecture imposes no checks that lower-privilege modes have XLEN settings less than or equal to the next-higher privilege mode; machine operation is well-defined even in this case.
- Some HINT instructions are encoded as integer computational instructions that overwrite their destination register with its current value, e.g., c.addi x8, 0.
- When such a HINT is executed with XLEN < MXLEN and bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register not all equal to bit XLEN-1, it is implementation-defined whether bits MXLEN..XLEN of the destination register are unchanged or are overwritten with copies of bit XLEN-1.
- This definition allows implementations to elide register write-back for some HINTs, while allowing them to execute other HINTs in the same manner as other integer computational instructions.
- The implementation choice is observable only by privilege modes with an XLEN setting greater than the current XLEN; it is invisible to the current privilege mode.

The following description:

- MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) modifies the effective privilege mode, i.e., the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute. When MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode. When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current privilege mode were set to MPP. Instruction address-translation and protection are unaffected by the setting of MPRV. MPRV is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.

- An MRET or SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets MPRV=0.

- The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by MPP might have different XLEN settings. When MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to MPP's XLEN, following the rules in Section 3.1.6.3.

- MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory. When MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1 in Figure 67) will succeed. When MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable or executable (R=1 or X=1) will succeed. MXR has no effect when pagebased virtual memory is not in effect. MXR is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported.

- SUM (permit Supervisor User Memory access) modifies the privilege with which S-mode loads and stores access virtual memory. When SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1 in Figure 67) will fault. When SUM=1, these accesses are permitted. SUM has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect. Note that, while SUM is ordinarily ignored when not executing in S-mode, it is in effect when MPRV=1 and MPP=S. SUM is read-only 0 if S-mode is not supported or if satp.MODE is read-only 0.

- The MXR and SUM mechanisms only affect the interpretation of permissions encoded in page-table entries. In particular, they have no impact on whether access-fault exceptions are raised due to PMAs or PMP.

Figure 67: not included in the provided text.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: mstatus MPRV (Modify PRiVilege) modifies the effective privilege mode, which is the privilege level at which explicit memory accesses execute.
RULE 2: When mstatus MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses behave as normal, using the translation and protection mechanisms of the current privilege mode.
RULE 3: When mstatus MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are translated and protected as though the current privilege mode were set to the privilege mode specified by mstatus MPP.
RULE 4: When mstatus MPRV=1, endianness is applied as though the current privilege mode were set to the privilege mode specified by mstatus MPP.
RULE 5: The setting of mstatus MPRV does not affect instruction address-translation and protection.
RULE 6: mstatus MPRV is read-only 0 if U-mode is not supported.
RULE 7: An MRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets mstatus MPRV to 0.
RULE 8: An SRET instruction that changes the privilege mode to a mode less privileged than M also sets mstatus MPRV to 0.
RULE 9: The current privilege mode and the privilege mode specified by mstatus MPP might have different XLEN settings.
RULE 10: When mstatus MPRV=1, load and store memory addresses are treated as though the current XLEN were set to the XLEN of the privilege mode specified by mstatus MPP.
RULE 11: mstatus MXR (Make eXecutable Readable) modifies the privilege with which loads access virtual memory.
RULE 12: When mstatus MXR=0, only loads from pages marked readable (R=1) will succeed.
RULE 13: When mstatus MXR=1, loads from pages marked either readable (R=1) or executable (X=1) will succeed.
RULE 14: mstatus MXR has no effect when page-based virtual memory is not in effect.

RULE 17: When mstatus SUM=0, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1) will fault.
RULE 18: When mstatus SUM=1, S-mode memory accesses to pages that are accessible by U-mode (U=1) are permitted.

The following description:

- The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus and mstatush are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches. Instruction fetches are always little-endian.
- MBE controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode (assuming mstatus.MPRV=0) are little-endian (MBE=0) or big-endian (MBE=1).
- If S-mode is not supported, SBE is read-only 0.
- Otherwise, SBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian (SBE=0) or big-endian (SBE=1).
- If U-mode is not supported, UBE is read-only 0.
- Otherwise, UBE controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian (UBE=0) or big-endian (UBE=1).
- For implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, such as page tables, endianness is always controlled by SBE.
- Since changing SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of these data structures, if any such data structures remain in use across a change to SBE, M-mode software must follow such a change to SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
- If S-mode is supported, an implementation may make SBE be a read-only copy of MBE.
- If U-mode is supported, an implementation may make UBE be a read-only copy of either MBE or SBE.
- An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if fields MBE, SBE, and UBE are all read-only 0.
- An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses (aside from instruction fetches) if MBE is read-only 1 and SBE and UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The MBE, SBE, and UBE bits in mstatus are WARL fields that control the endianness of memory accesses other than instruction fetches.
RULE 2: The mstatus.MBE bit controls whether non-instruction-fetch memory accesses made from M-mode while mstatus.MPRV=0 are little-endian when mstatus.MBE=0 or big-endian when mstatus.MBE=1.
RULE 3: If S-mode is not supported, the mstatus.SBE bit is read-only 0.
RULE 4: If S-mode is supported, the mstatus.SBE bit controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from S-mode are little-endian when mstatus.SBE=0 or big-endian when mstatus.SBE=1.
RULE 5: If U-mode is not supported, the mstatus.UBE bit is read-only 0.
RULE 6: If U-mode is supported, the mstatus.UBE bit controls whether explicit load and store memory accesses made from U-mode are little-endian when mstatus.UBE=0 or big-endian when mstatus.UBE=1.
RULE 7: The mstatus.SBE bit always controls the endianness of implicit accesses to supervisor-level memory management data structures, such as page tables.
RULE 8: Because a change to mstatus.SBE alters the implementation's interpretation of supervisor-level memory management data structures, if any supervisor-level memory management data structures remain in use across a change to mstatus.SBE, M-mode software must follow that change to mstatus.SBE by executing an SFENCE.VMA instruction with rs1 = x0 and rs2 = x0.
RULE 9: An implementation supports only little-endian memory accesses if the MBE, SBE, and UBE fields in mstatus are all read-only 0.
RULE 10: An implementation supports only big-endian memory accesses aside from instruction fetches if mstatus.MBE is read-only 1, and mstatus.SBE and mstatus.UBE are each read-only 1 when S-mode and U-mode are supported.

The following description:

3.1.6.6. Virtualization Support in mstatus Register

- The TVM (Trap Virtual Memory) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting supervisor virtual-memory management operations.
- When TVM=1, attempts to read or write the satp CSR or execute an SFENCE.VMA or SINVAL.VMA instruction while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- When TVM=0, these operations are permitted in S-mode.
- TVM is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.
- The TW (Timeout Wait) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the WFI instruction (see Section 3.3.3).
- When TW=0, the WFI instruction may execute in modes less privileged than M when not prevented for some other reason.
- When TW=1, then if WFI is executed in any less-privileged mode, and it does not complete within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit, the WFI instruction causes an illegal-instruction exception.
- An implementation may have WFI always raise an illegal-instruction exception in modes less privileged than M when TW=1, even if there are pending globally-disabled interrupts when the instruction is executed.
- TW is read-only 0 when there are no modes less privileged than M.
- When S-mode is implemented, then executing WFI in U-mode causes an illegal-instruction exception, regardless of the value of the TW bit, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time limit.
- The TSR (Trap SRET) bit is a WARL field that supports intercepting the supervisor exception return instruction, SRET.
- When TSR=1, attempts to execute SRET while executing in S-mode will raise an illegal-instruction exception.
- When TSR=0, this operation is permitted in S-mode.
- TSR is read-only 0 when S-mode is not supported.
============================================================

The following description:

- The FS[1:0] and VS[1:0] WARL fields and the XS[1:0] read-only field are used to reduce the cost of context save and restore by setting and tracking the current state of the floating-point unit and any other user-mode extensions respectively.
- The FS field encodes the status of the floating-point unit state, including the floating-point registers f0 -f31 and the CSRs fcsr , frm , and fflags .
- The VS field encodes the status of the vector extension state, including the vector registers v0 -v31 and the CSRs vcsr , vxrm , vxsat , vstart , vl , vtype , and vlenb .
- The XS field encodes the status of additional user-mode extensions and associated state.
- These fields can be checked by a context switch routine to quickly determine whether a state save or restore is required.
- If a save or restore is required, additional instructions and CSRs are typically required to effect and optimize the process.
- The design anticipates that most context switches will not need to save/restore state in either or both of the floating-point unit or other extensions, so provides a fast check via the SD bit.
- The FS, VS, and XS fields use the same status encoding as shown in Table 13, with the four possible status values being Off, Initial, Clean, and Dirty.

Table 13. Encoding of FS[1:0], VS[1:0], and XS[1:0] status fields

| Status | FS and VS Meaning | XS Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| 0 | Off | All off |
| 1 | Initial | None dirty or clean, some on |
| 2 | Clean | None dirty, some clean |
| 3 | Dirty | Some dirty |

- If the F extension is implemented, the FS field shall not be read-only zero.
- If neither the F extension nor S-mode is implemented, then FS is read-only zero.
- If S-mode is implemented but the F extension is not, FS may optionally be read-only zero.
- Implementations with S-mode but without the F extension are permitted, but not required, to make the FS field be read-only zero.
- Some such implementations will choose not to have the FS field be read-only zero, so as to enable emulation of the F extension for both S-mode and Umode via invisible traps into M-mode.
- If the v registers are implemented, the VS field shall not be read-only zero.
- If neither the v registers nor S-mode is implemented, then VS is read-only zero.
- If S-mode is implemented but the v registers are not, VS may optionally be read-only zero.
- In harts without additional user extensions requiring new state, the XS field is read-only zero.
- Every additional extension with state provides a CSR field that encodes the equivalent of the XS states.
- The XS field represents a summary of all extensions' status as shown in Table 13.
- The XS field effectively reports the maximum status value across all user-extension status fields, though individual extensions can use a different encoding than XS.
- The XS field provides a summary of all added extension state, but additional microarchitectural bits might be maintained in the extension to further reduce context save and restore overhead.
- The SD bit is a read-only bit that summarizes whether either the FS, VS, or XS fields signal the presence of some dirty state that will require saving extended user context to memory.
- If FS, XS, and VS are all readonly zero, then SD is also always zero.
- When an extension's status is set to Off, any instruction that attempts to read or write the corresponding state will cause an illegal-instruction exception.
- When the status is Initial, the corresponding state should have an initial constant value.
- When the status is Clean, the corresponding state is potentially different from the initial value, but matches the last value stored on a context swap.
- When the status is Dirty, the corresponding state has potentially been modified since the last context save.
- During a context save, the responsible privileged code need only write out the corresponding state if its status is Dirty, and can then reset the extension's status to Clean.
- During a context restore, the context need only be loaded from memory if the status is Clean (it should never be Dirty at restore).
- If the status is Initial, the context must be set to an initial constant value on context restore to avoid a security hole, but this can be done without accessing memory.
- For example, the floating-point registers can all be initialized to the immediate value 0.
- The FS and XS fields are read by the privileged code before saving the context.
- The FS field is set directly by privileged code when resuming a user context, while the XS field is set indirectly by writing to the status register of the individual extensions.
- The status fields will also be updated during execution of instructions, regardless of privilege mode.
- For example, a coprocessor might require to be configured before use and can be "unconfigured" after use.
- The unconfigured state would be represented as the Initial state for context save.
- If the same application remains running between the unconfigure and the next configure (which would set status to Dirty), there is no need to actually reinitialize the state at the unconfigure instruction, as all state is local to the user process, i.e., the Initial state may only cause the coprocessor state to be initialized to a constant value at context restore, not at every unconfigure.
- Executing a user-mode instruction to disable a unit and place it into the Off state will cause an illegal-instruction exception to be raised if any subsequent instruction tries to use the unit before it is turned back on.
- A user-mode instruction to turn a unit on must also ensure the unit's state is properly initialized, as the unit might have been used by another context meantime.
- Changing the setting of FS has no effect on the contents of the floating-point register state.
- In particular, setting FS=Off does not destroy the state, nor does setting FS=Initial clear the contents.
- Similarly, the setting of VS has no effect on the contents of the vector register state.
- Other extensions, however, might not preserve state when set to Off.
- Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the floating-point register state imprecisely by reporting the state to be dirty even when it has not been modified.
- On some implementations, some instructions that do not mutate the floating-point state may cause the state to transition from Initial or Clean to Dirty.
- On other implementations, dirtiness might not be tracked at all, in which case the valid FS states are Off and Dirty, and an attempt to set FS to Initial or Clean causes it to be set to Dirty.
- This definition of FS does not disallow setting FS to Dirty as a result of errant speculation.
- Some platforms may choose to disallow speculatively writing FS to close a potential side channel.
- If an instruction explicitly or implicitly writes a floating-point register or the fcsr but does not alter its contents, and FS=Initial or FS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether FS transitions to Dirty.
- Implementations may choose to track the dirtiness of the vector register state in an analogous imprecise fashion, including possibly setting VS to Dirty when software attempts to set VS=Initial or VS=Clean.
- When VS=Initial or VS=Clean, it is implementation-defined whether an instruction that writes a vector register or vector CSR but does not alter its contents causes VS to transition to Dirty.
- Table 14 shows all the possible state transitions for the FS, VS, or XS status bits.
- Note that the standard floating-point and vector extensions do not support user-mode unconfigure or disable/enable instructions.

Table 14. FS, VS, and XS state transitions.

| Current State Action | Off | Initial | Clean | Dirty |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code | At context save in privileged code |
| Save state? Next state | No Off | No Initial | No Clean | Yes Clean |
| At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code | At context restore in privileged code |
| Restore state? Next state | No Off | Yes, to initial Initial | Yes, from memory Clean | N/A N/A |
| Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state | Execute instruction to read state |
| Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Initial | Execute Clean | Execute Dirty |
| Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration | Execute instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration |
| Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Dirty | Execute Dirty | Execute Dirty |
| Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit | Execute instruction to unconfigure unit |
| Action? Next state | Exception Off | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial |
| Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit | Execute instruction to disable unit |
| Action? Next state | Execute Off | Execute Off | Execute Off | Execute Off |
| Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit | Execute instruction to enable unit |
| Action? Next state | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial | Execute Initial |

- The SD bit is read-only and is set when either the FS, VS, or XS bits encode a Dirty state (i.e., SD=(FS==0b11 OR XS==0b11 OR VS==0b11) ).
- This allows privileged code to quickly determine when no additional context save is required beyond the integer register set and pc .
- Machine and Supervisor modes share a single copy of the FS, VS, and XS bits.
- Supervisor-level software normally uses the FS, VS, and XS bits directly to record the status with respect to the supervisor-level saved context.
- Machine-level software must be more conservative in saving and restoring the extension state in their corresponding version of the context.
============================================================

RULE 78: During a context restore in privileged code, when the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Off, no state is restored and the next state is Off.
RULE 79: During a context restore in privileged code, when the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Initial, state is restored to initial and the next state is Initial.
RULE 80: During a context restore in privileged code, when the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Clean, state is restored from memory and the next state is Clean.
RULE 81: During a context restore in privileged code, when the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Dirty, the restore action and next state are not applicable.
RULE 82: When an instruction executes to read state and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Off, an exception occurs and the next state is Off.
RULE 83: When an instruction executes to read state and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 84: When an instruction executes to read state and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Clean.
RULE 85: When an instruction executes to read state and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 86: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Off, an exception occurs and the next state is Off.
RULE 87: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 88: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 89: When an instruction that possibly modifies state, including configuration, executes and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Dirty.
RULE 90: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Off, an exception occurs and the next state is Off.
RULE 91: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Initial, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 92: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Clean, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 93: When an instruction executes to unconfigure a unit and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Dirty, the instruction executes and the next state is Initial.
RULE 94: When an instruction executes to disable a unit and the mstatus FS, VS, or XS status is Off, the instruction executes and the next state is Off.

The following description:

- An interrupt i will trap to M-mode (causing the privilege mode to change to M-mode) if all of the following are true: (a) either the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set, or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode; (b) bit i is set in both mip and mie; and (c) if register mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.

- These conditions for an interrupt trap to occur must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt becomes, or ceases to be, pending in mip, and must also be evaluated immediately following the execution of an x RET instruction or an explicit write to a CSR on which these interrupt trap conditions expressly depend (including mip, mie, mstatus, and mideleg).
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: An interrupt i will trap to M-mode if all of the following are true: either the current privilege mode is M and the MIE bit in the mstatus register is set or the current privilege mode has less privilege than M-mode; bit i is set in both mip and mie; and if register mideleg exists, bit i is not set in mideleg.
RULE 2: The interrupt trap conditions that expressly depend on the mstatus register must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt becomes pending in mip.
RULE 3: The interrupt trap conditions that expressly depend on the mstatus register must be evaluated in a bounded amount of time from when an interrupt ceases to be pending in mip.
RULE 4: The interrupt trap conditions that expressly depend on the mstatus register must be evaluated immediately following the execution of an x RET instruction.
RULE 5: The interrupt trap conditions that expressly depend on the mstatus register must be evaluated immediately following an explicit write to any CSR on which the interrupt trap conditions expressly depend, including mip, mie, mstatus, and mideleg.

The following description:

- For the Wait for Interrupt instruction (WFI): "This instruction may raise an illegal-instruction exception when TW=1 in mstatus, as described in Section 3.1.6.6."

- "The operation of WFI must be unaffected by the global interrupt bits in mstatus (MIE and SIE) and the delegation register mideleg (i.e., the hart must resume if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending, even if it has been delegated to a lessprivileged mode), but should honor the individual interrupt enables (e.g, MTIE) (i.e., implementations should avoid resuming the hart if the interrupt is pending but not individually enabled)."

RESPONSE:
=========

RULE 2: The operation of the Wait for Interrupt instruction must be unaffected by the mstatus register's global interrupt bits MIE and SIE.
RULE 3: The hart must resume from the Wait for Interrupt instruction if a locally enabled interrupt becomes pending regardless of the mstatus register's global interrupt bits MIE and SIE.
RULE 4: The Wait for Interrupt instruction is required to resume execution for a locally enabled interrupt pending at any privilege level regardless of the mstatus register's global interrupt enable at each privilege level.

The following description:

- The hypervisor extension adds two fields, MPV and GVA, to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR, and modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.
- When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds MPV and GVA not to mstatus but to mstatush. The format of mstatus is unchanged for RV32.
- The MPV bit (Machine Previous Virtualization Mode) is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
- For a trap taken into M-mode, the source states: "Just as the MPP field is set to the (nominal) privilege mode at the time of the trap, the MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap."
- When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to MPV, unless MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.
- Field GVA (Guest Virtual Address) is written by the implementation whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
- For any trap (breakpoint, address misaligned, access fault, page fault, or guest-page fault) that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, GVA is set to 1.
- For any other trap into M-mode, GVA is set to 0.
- The TSR and TVM fields of mstatus affect execution only in HS-mode, not in VS-mode.
- The TW field affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
- Setting TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp or executing HFENCE.GVMA or HINVAL.GVMA, but has no effect on accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA.
- Setting TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp or instructions HFENCE.VVMA or HINVAL.VVMA, or for any actions taken in VS-mode.
- The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the Modify Privilege field, MPRV, of mstatus.
- When MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal.
- When MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied, as though the current virtualization mode were set to MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to MPP.
- Table 49 enumerates the cases.

Table 49. Effect of MPRV on the translation and protection of explicit memory accesses.

| MPRV | MPV | MPP | Effect |
|------|-----|-----|--------|
| 0 | - | - | Normal access; current privilege mode applies. |
| 1 | 0 | 0 | U-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | 0 | 1 | HS-level access with HS-level translation and protection only. |
| 1 | - | 3 | M-level access with no translation. |
| 1 | 1 | 0 | VU-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus .MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. |
| 1 | 1 | 1 | VS-level access with two-stage translation and protection. The HS-levelMXR bit makes any executable page readable. vsstatus .MXR makes readable those pages marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if readable at the guest-physical translation stage. vsstatus .SUM applies instead of the HS- level SUMbit. |

- MPRV does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions, HLV, HLVX, and HSV.
- The explicit loads and stores of these instructions always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus .SPVP, overriding MPRV.
- The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: The hypervisor extension adds the MPV bit, Machine Previous Virtualization Mode, to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR.
RULE 2: The hypervisor extension adds the GVA field, Guest Virtual Address, to the machine-level mstatus or mstatush CSR.
RULE 3: The hypervisor extension modifies the behavior of several existing mstatus fields.
RULE 4: When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds MPV to mstatush and not to mstatus.
RULE 5: When MXLEN=32, the hypervisor extension adds GVA to mstatush and not to mstatus.
RULE 6: For RV32, the format of mstatus is unchanged.
RULE 7: The implementation writes the mstatus MPV bit whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 8: For a trap taken into M-mode, the mstatus MPV bit is set to the value of the virtualization mode V at the time of the trap.
RULE 9: For a trap taken into M-mode, the mstatus MPP field is set to the nominal privilege mode at the time of the trap.
RULE 10: When an MRET instruction is executed, the virtualization mode V is set to mstatus.MPV unless mstatus.MPP=3, in which case V remains 0.
RULE 11: The implementation writes the mstatus GVA field whenever a trap is taken into M-mode.
RULE 12: For a breakpoint, address-misaligned, access-fault, page-fault, or guest-page-fault trap taken into M-mode that writes a guest virtual address to mtval, the mstatus GVA field is set to 1.
RULE 13: For any other trap taken into M-mode, the mstatus GVA field is set to 0.
RULE 14: The mstatus TSR field affects execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.
RULE 15: The mstatus TVM field affects execution only in HS-mode and not in VS-mode.
RULE 16: The mstatus TW field affects execution in all modes except M-mode.
RULE 17: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from accessing hgatp.
RULE 18: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from executing HFENCE.GVMA.
RULE 19: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 prevents HS-mode from executing HINVAL.GVMA.
RULE 20: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 has no effect on accesses to vsatp.
RULE 21: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 has no effect on execution of HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 22: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 has no effect on execution of HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 23: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for accesses to vsatp.
RULE 24: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for execution of HFENCE.VVMA.
RULE 25: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for execution of HINVAL.VVMA.
RULE 26: Setting mstatus.TVM=1 does not cause traps for any actions taken in VS-mode.
RULE 27: The hypervisor extension changes the behavior of the mstatus MPRV field.
RULE 28: The mstatus MPRV field is the Modify Privilege field.
RULE 29: When mstatus.MPRV=0, translation and protection behave as normal.
RULE 30: When mstatus.MPRV=0, explicit memory accesses are normal accesses and the current privilege mode applies.
RULE 31: When mstatus.MPRV=1, explicit memory accesses are translated and protected, and endianness is applied as though the current virtualization mode were set to mstatus.MPV and the current nominal privilege mode were set to mstatus.MPP.
RULE 32: When mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=0, and mstatus.MPP=0, explicit memory accesses are U-level accesses with HS-level translation and protection only.
RULE 33: When mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=0, and mstatus.MPP=1, explicit memory accesses are HS-level accesses with HS-level translation and protection only.
RULE 34: When mstatus.MPRV=1 and mstatus.MPP=3, explicit memory accesses are M-level accesses with no translation regardless of mstatus.MPV.
RULE 35: When mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=0, explicit memory accesses are VU-level accesses with two-stage translation and protection.
RULE 36: For explicit memory accesses, when mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=0, the HS-level MXR bit in mstatus makes any executable page readable.
RULE 37: For explicit memory accesses, when mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=0, vsstatus.MXR makes readable pages that are marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if those pages are readable at the guest-physical translation stage.
RULE 38: When mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, explicit memory accesses are VS-level accesses with two-stage translation and protection.
RULE 39: For explicit memory accesses, when mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, the HS-level MXR bit in mstatus makes any executable page readable.
RULE 40: For explicit memory accesses, when mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, vsstatus.MXR makes readable pages that are marked executable at the VS translation stage, but only if those pages are readable at the guest-physical translation stage.
RULE 41: For explicit memory accesses, when mstatus.MPRV=1, mstatus.MPV=1, and mstatus.MPP=1, vsstatus.SUM applies instead of the HS-level SUM bit.
RULE 42: The mstatus MPRV field does not affect the virtual-machine load/store instructions HLV, HLVX, and HSV.
RULE 43: The explicit loads and stores of HLV, HLVX, and HSV always act as though V=1 and the nominal privilege mode were hstatus.SPVP, overriding mstatus.MPRV.
RULE 44: The mstatus register is a superset of the HS-level sstatus register but is not a superset of vsstatus.

The following description:

- An instruction is HS-qualified if it would be valid to execute in HS-mode (for some values of the instruction's register operands), assuming fields TSR and TVM of CSR mstatus are both zero.

- Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following case: in VS-mode or VU-mode, attempts to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR or VS CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

- Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following case: in VS-mode or VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR or high-half VS CSR when the same access (read/write) to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

- Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following case: in VU-mode, attempts to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0, or to execute a supervisor instruction (SRET or SFENCE).

- Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following case: in VU-mode, attempts to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR when the same access (read/write) would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

- Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following case: in VU-mode, if XLEN=32, attempts to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR when the same access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode, assuming mstatus.TVM=0.

- Specifically, a virtual-instruction exception is raised for the following case: in VS-mode, attempts to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific, bounded time.

- Because TSR and TVM in mstatus are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode), they are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
RULE 1: In determining whether an instruction is HS-qualified, mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM are both assumed zero when considering whether the instruction would be valid to execute in HS-mode for some values of the instruction's register operands.
RULE 2: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode for an attempt to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR.
RULE 3: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode for an attempt to access an implemented non-high-half VS CSR.
RULE 4: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode for an attempt to access an implemented non-high-half hypervisor CSR.
RULE 5: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode for an attempt to access an implemented non-high-half VS CSR.
RULE 6: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode with XLEN=32 for an attempt to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR.
RULE 7: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode with XLEN=32 for an attempt to access an implemented high-half VS CSR.
RULE 8: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode with XLEN=32 for an attempt to access an implemented high-half hypervisor CSR.
RULE 9: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode with XLEN=32 for an attempt to access an implemented high-half VS CSR.
RULE 10: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode for an attempt to execute WFI when mstatus.TW=0.
RULE 11: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode for an attempt to access an implemented non-high-half supervisor CSR.
RULE 12: mstatus.TVM is assumed zero when evaluating whether the same read or write access to the CSR's low-half partner would be allowed in HS-mode for determining whether a virtual-instruction exception is raised in VU-mode with XLEN=32 for an attempt to access an implemented high-half supervisor CSR.
RULE 13: A virtual-instruction exception is raised in VS-mode for an attempt to execute WFI when hstatus.VTW=1 and mstatus.TW=0, unless the instruction completes within an implementation-specific bounded time.
RULE 14: mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM are intended to impact only S-mode (HS-mode).
RULE 15: mstatus.TSR and mstatus.TVM are ignored for determining exceptions in VS-mode.
